# Structure v2 proposal

Status: revision 5, 2026-09-26. Revision 4 reached CONSENSUS in Codex
adversarial review round 9 (`design/round-9-codex.md`) after REVISE verdicts
in rounds 6 to 8. End-to-end tests of the first implementation then showed
that the skill's reference reads were unreliable, so revision 5 embeds the
upstream texts instead. It goes to review round 10. See "Revision 5" at the
end. Research is in `design/research/`, summarized in
`design/research/00-synthesis.md`. Test results are in
`design/research/07-tests-codex.md`, `08-tests-claude.md`,
`09-tests-clear-compact.md`, `10-tests-hook-env.md`, and
`11-tests-e2e.md`. D1 to D21 refer to
`design/DECISIONS.md` as of `fc308bd`.

## Goals

The user set these goals:

- a clean marketplace
- clean skills
- easy to maintain
- supported by both runtimes' documented behavior
- a proven structure, one that popular repos already use
- easy to extend, starting with a planned `presentation` plugin that reuses
  i-have-adhd
- automation in GitHub Actions that keeps the repo working, clean, and
  current

## Summary of changes

1. `adhd-unslop` becomes self-contained. Its skill embeds both upstream
   texts in its body, the same bytes the always-on hook delivers. The Claude
   Code `dependencies` field, the dependency-check hook, and
   `hooks/dependencies.json` go away.
2. `au-i-have-adhd` and `au-unslop` stay as optional standalone mirrors.
   Each ships the upstream `SKILL.md` byte for byte, frontmatter included.
3. The always-on hook keeps its three embedded chunks and its hook
   definitions, so Codex trust carries over. The launcher skips `resume` in
   Claude Code only, the chunk text becomes factual, and an environment
   variable can switch injection off.
4. The build becomes generic. Every file under `plugins/` is generated from
   `tools/plugins.json`, `src/<plugin>/`, and `upstream/`. The check fails on
   stray files. Only `--prune` deletes them.
5. `adhd-unslop` gains a user-only `doctor` skill.
6. CI adds keyless install and load tests in both CLIs with pinned
   versions, a version gate, workflow linting, a daily run against the
   latest CLI releases, and Dependabot with actions pinned by commit.

## Decisions

### P1. Self-contained adhd-unslop, no plugin dependencies

This reverses part of D4 and all of D15, which the user chose over an earlier
proposal to copy the texts at build time. Three findings changed the
balance.

- No repo out of 25 uses plugin `dependencies`. Repos that share text
  between plugins copy it at build time and check the copies for drift
  (`03-multi-plugin-marketplaces.md`).
- Dependencies failed in practice. `claude plugin update` did not install
  newly declared dependencies, and a repeated install added one missing
  dependency per run (D16). Codex has no dependencies at all, so Codex users
  ran three install commands and relied on a warning hook.
- The hook already carries its own copy of both texts (D12), so a skill that
  carries the same copy adds no new kind of duplication.

What the user asked for still holds. The text comes only from our own pinned
copies, never a third-party marketplace. i-have-adhd stays available as its
own plugin. A new plugin reuses i-have-adhd by listing it, and the build
copies the text in.

The skill embeds the texts. Its body, after a one-line generated comment,
is the always-on bundle byte for byte: the overlay sections, the
i-have-adhd block, the unslop block, and the final check. It reads no files.

- Revision 4 had the skill read the texts from `references/` files next to
  it. End-to-end tests showed that failed in both runtimes (`11`). Claude
  Code denied both reads in `-p` mode because the files sit outside the
  working directory, and an interactive session would ask for permission
  each time. Codex skipped the reads in 7 of 8 runs with a short prompt.
  Rules that must always apply cannot depend on a permission rule or on the
  model choosing to read a file.
- `references/` is the progressive-disclosure pattern for optional detail.
  These texts are the rules themselves, so they belong in the body. 0.1.0
  embedded them the same way, and `design/BEHAVIOR.md` recorded that it
  worked.
- `allowed-tools: Read` in the frontmatter would let Claude Code read
  without asking, but it would pre-approve every Read while the skill is
  active and would do nothing for Codex. Rejected.
- Size: about 21,800 bytes, about 5,500 tokens. Codex cuts skills at 8,000
  bytes only for a plugin with a root `plugin.json` that uses the
  agent-plugins `$schema`, and our plugins have none (`07` C1). The
  end-to-end check confirms that the whole skill arrives in both runtimes
  (`11`).
- A user who invokes the skill while always-on already delivered the bundle
  gets a second, identical copy. That costs tokens and changes nothing,
  because the bundle rules treat a repeat as a repeat. The case is rare,
  since always-on makes invoking unnecessary.

Result: `claude plugin install adhd-unslop@adhd-unslop` or
`codex plugin add adhd-unslop@adhd-unslop` installs everything. Updates touch
one plugin.

#### Embedding contract

- A skill template embeds an upstream with a line `{{upstream <name>}}`.
  The build replaces it with the upstream block: the line `<!-- BEGIN
  upstream <name> <repo>/<path> @<commit> -->`, the upstream `SKILL.md`
  with its frontmatter removed and a final newline ensured, and `<!-- END
  upstream <name> -->`.
- The always-on chunks use the same block, byte for byte.
- `tools/plugins.json` lists each skill's embedded upstreams in
  `upstreams`. The build fails when the template's directives and the list
  disagree, or when a directive names an unpinned upstream.
- Tests check that each listed block appears exactly once in the skill and
  matches the hook chunk that carries it, and that the `adhd-unslop` skill
  body equals the hook bundle.

#### Versioning rule

A plugin ships an upstream's text when it is that upstream's vendored
mirror, when one of its skills embeds the upstream, or when its always-on
chunks include it. `sync.mjs` raises the patch version of every such plugin
once per bump run. A future `presentation` plugin that embeds i-have-adhd
therefore gets a new version whenever i-have-adhd changes. The CI version
gate catches every other change to shipped files.

### P2. Vendored plugins are byte-for-byte mirrors

`au-i-have-adhd` and `au-unslop` ship the upstream `SKILL.md` unchanged,
frontmatter included. Neither upstream skill folder has an
`agents/openai.yaml`, so the build generates one that matches the Claude Code
setting: `allow_implicit_invocation: false` when the upstream frontmatter
sets `disable-model-invocation: true`, and `true` otherwise.

- This reverses D7, whose only reason was that `adhd-unslop` loaded these
  skills through the Skill tool. Nothing loads them now.
- A mirror that changes nothing is simpler to trust and to test. The test
  becomes one comparison of the whole file.
- Typed invocation still works. i-have-adhd stays user-only, as upstream
  ships it. unslop stays model-invocable, as upstream ships it.
- The `au-` prefix stays (D6). The name clash it prevents still exists.
- The plugins are optional. `adhd-unslop` never needs them.

### P3. The always-on hook keeps embedded chunks

The hook keeps three chunks printed from files inside `adhd-unslop`.

- Always-on SessionStart injection is the proven pattern: superpowers,
  ponytail, caveman, ECC, and planning-with-files use it.
- Codex has no other mechanism a plugin can set. `~/.codex/AGENTS.md` and
  `developer_instructions` belong to the user (`06-official-docs.md`).
- Three chunks are needed because Claude Code saves a value over 10,000
  characters to a file and shows the model a path and the first 2,000
  characters (`08` L5). The cap is documented at
  code.claude.com/docs/en/hooks.md#json-output and cannot be raised.

Changes:

- The matcher stays `startup|resume|clear|compact`, and the launcher
  decides what to do on `resume`.
  - Claude Code keeps the earlier injection in a resumed session, so a
    second copy of about 24,000 characters arrived on every resume (`08`
    L1). Claude Code never loses the copy: `/compact`, `/clear`, and
    auto-compaction each leave exactly one (`09`). The launcher prints
    nothing on `resume` in Claude Code.
  - Codex also duplicates the copy on resume (`07` C4), but it can lose the
    copy too. Codex fires `compact` only when the next turn starts, so a
    user who compacts and quits before sending a message resumes later with
    no copy (`09`). The launcher keeps injecting on `resume` in Codex. A
    duplicate costs tokens, and a missing copy silently turns always-on
    off, so the duplicate is the lesser problem. This is today's behavior.
  - The launcher tells the runtimes apart by `PLUGIN_ROOT`, which Codex sets
    and Claude Code never does (`10`). When it cannot tell, it injects.
  - It reads `source` from the SessionStart payload on stdin, with a short
    timeout, and injects when stdin is missing or unreadable.
  - A forked Claude Code session sends `source: "fork"` and carries the
    parent's copy (`08` L1), so `fork` needs no match.
  - One Claude Code edge case: a user who turns always-on on during a
    session and then resumes it gets no copy until the next startup,
    `/clear`, or compaction. The README says to start a new session.
  - Claude Code orders the chunks by which handler finishes first, while
    Codex keeps the declared order (`09`). The header already accepts any
    order.
- Handlers keep `additionalContextLimit: 5000`. The built chunks were not
  tested against the Codex default with non-ASCII text, and unslop contains
  `→`. Keeping the key costs nothing and avoids that risk.
- Header wording becomes statements of fact. The Claude Code docs warn that
  imperative hook text can trigger prompt-injection defenses. The header
  adds that a chunk counts only when its END line is present.
- The Codex manifest names the hooks file explicitly, as every repo that
  ships a Codex plugin hook does. Trust survives the explicit path (`07` C3).

`ADHD_UNSLOP_ALWAYS` semantics:

- Unset or empty: the flag files decide, as today.
- `0`, `false`, or `off`, in any case: injection is off for this process,
  even when a flag file exists.
- `1`, `true`, or `on`: injection is on without a flag file.
- Any other value is treated as unset. The doctor reports it.

Codex keys hook trust by plugin, hooks file path, event, group, and handler
index, and checks a hash of the handler fields (`07` C3). The three chunk
handlers keep their group, positions, and every field, so trust recorded
for 0.2.2 stays valid and Codex users do not review the hooks again. The
dependency check was the second group, so removing it shifts nothing. A
unit test freezes the three handler definitions, so a change to them fails
until someone decides to accept one more review.

The chunk footer also names the environment switch, so it no longer tells
a user that removing the flag files is the only way off.

### P4. Output styles are not adopted

A plugin can force an output style with `force-for-plugin: true`. It
arrives with no size cap and persists through compaction (`08` L8, docs). It
is rejected:

- It overrides the user's own `outputStyle` whenever the plugin is enabled,
  which bypasses the opt-in flag (D14).
- No repo of the 25 ships one, so it is not proven.
- It is Claude Code only. Codex still needs the hook, so the repo would
  maintain two delivery paths.

Revisit if Claude Code adds an opt-in style that composes with the user's
style.

### P5. No per-session mode tracker

caveman stores mode state per session so compaction cannot turn a mode back
on. That needs a `UserPromptSubmit` hook on every prompt, in both runtimes,
with its own trust entry in Codex. The case it fixes, a user stopping a mode
and then compacting, is rare, and a repeated stop command fixes it. The
limitation stays documented.

### P6. Every file under plugins/ is generated

`tools/plugins.json` lists each plugin. Its schema:

| Field | Kinds | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `name`, `version`, `category`, `description`, `keywords` | all | Manifest fields |
| `marketplaceDescription` | all, optional | Marketplace listing text |
| `codexInterface` | all | Codex `interface` block |
| `kind` | all | `vendored` or `authored` |
| `upstream`, `skill` | vendored | The pinned upstream and its skill folder name |
| `skills` | authored | Map of skill name to `{ "upstreams": [upstream, ...] }`, the upstreams its template embeds |
| `alwaysOn` | authored, optional | Ordered chunk list. Each chunk lists parts: `{ "file": "<path under src/<plugin>/>" }` or `{ "upstream": "<name>" }` |

An authored plugin's hand-written sources live in `src/<plugin>/`:

- `skills/<skill>/` is copied to `plugins/<plugin>/skills/<skill>/`. A
  `SKILL.md.tmpl` in it is rendered to `SKILL.md`. The template syntax is
  two line directives: `{{include <path under src/<plugin>/>}}` inserts
  that file, and `{{upstream <name>}}` inserts the pinned upstream block.
  gstack renders skills from templates the same way.
- `hooks/` is copied to `plugins/<plugin>/hooks/`.
- Generated per plugin: both manifests, `README.md`, `NOTICE.md`, one
  `LICENSES/<upstream>.LICENSE` per upstream it ships, and the always-on
  chunks with their manifest.
# Tests: end to end on the first 0.3.0 build

Date: 2026-09-26. Claude Code 2.1.283, Codex 0.154.0 and 0.157.1.
`tests/e2e/run.sh` with the modes `claude`, `codex`, and `migrate`, in
throwaway homes as `AGENTS.md` requires. The build under test was the
first implementation of `STRUCTURE-v2.md` revision 4, in which the
`adhd-unslop` skill read both upstream texts from files in its own
`references/` folder.

## Results

| Run | Passed |
| --- | --- |
| Claude Code 2.1.283 | 15 of 17 |
| Codex 0.154.0 | 17 of 18 in one run, 16 of 18 in the final run |
| Codex 0.157.1 | 16 of 18 |
| `migrate`, Claude Code and both Codex versions | 16 of 16 |

After every run a `find` showed no `auth.json`, no `shell_snapshots`, and
no key strings in the scratch folders.

## Findings

### The Claude Code reads were denied

In `claude -p`, both Read calls on `skills/adhd-unslop/references/*.md`
were denied in every run, because the files sit outside the working
directory. The model still answered as asked, with no upstream rules
loaded. An interactive session would ask the user for permission to read
each file instead.

- `allowed-tools: Read` in the skill's frontmatter let the reads through
  and passed `claude plugin validate --strict`. It would pre-approve every
  Read while the skill is active, and it does nothing for Codex.
- A narrower rule, `Read(${CLAUDE_SKILL_DIR}/references/**)`, did not work.

### Codex usually skipped the reads

With the prompt `$adhd-unslop:adhd-unslop Reply with the single word
READY.`, Codex read the reference files in 1 of 8 runs across both
versions. With the same prompt, 0.2.2, whose skill named other skills to
read, read them in 2 of 2 runs. With the plugin's own default prompt, `Use
$adhd-unslop:adhd-unslop for this session.`, it read both files in 4 of 4
runs. Whether the model reads a file depends on the prompt, so a rule set
that must always apply cannot rely on it.

### Codex sometimes loaded the unslop mirror unprompted

With both mirrors installed, an unrelated question made Codex 0.154.0 load
`au-unslop:unslop` in 2 of 4 runs. Codex 0.157.1 did not, in 4 of 4. The
mirror keeps upstream's frontmatter, whose description says "Must always
apply", and its `openai.yaml` allows implicit invocation to match
upstream (`DECISIONS.md` D7). A user who has always-on and this mirror gets
a second, independent unslop that the `stop unslop` switch does not
control.

### Migration

- Every `migrate` check passed in both runtimes: the 0.2.2 install, the
  update commands, `claude plugin prune`, Codex hook trust carried over
  without review, and one complete bundle after the update.
- After `claude plugin prune`, an `au-unslop` mirror that the user had
  installed by name stayed at 0.1.1. Codex moved it to 0.2.0. Anyone who
  keeps a mirror in Claude Code needs `claude plugin update
  au-unslop@adhd-unslop` as well.

## Conclusions

- Mandatory rules must not depend on the model choosing to read a file, or
  on a permission rule. `STRUCTURE-v2.md` revision 5 embeds both upstream
  texts in the `adhd-unslop` skill body instead of `references/`.
- The end-to-end checks should prove that text arrived in context, from the
  transcript or rollout, not infer it from how the model answers a short
  prompt.
- The unprompted-question check runs before the mirrors are installed, the
  clean case. The mirror's auto-load is documented in the README.
- The README's upgrade steps add `claude plugin update` for a kept mirror.

## An invoked skill after compaction in Claude Code

Date: 2026-09-26. Claude Code 2.1.283, revision 5 build, in which the
`adhd-unslop` skill embeds both upstream texts. `SKILL.md` is 21,823
characters, about 5,455 tokens at 4 characters per token, and its body after
the frontmatter is 21,356 characters. compound-engineering reads the docs as
saying that compaction re-attaches each invoked skill with only its first
5,000 tokens, 25,000 for all skills together. This test checks what our
skill keeps.

### Method

Always-on was off, so no hook text was in context. Each case used a
throwaway `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, as `tests/e2e/run.sh` does, which now runs
both cases and prints the result as INFO lines.

- Case A, one process. `tests/e2e/claude-turns.mjs` keeps one `claude -p
  --input-format stream-json` process open and sends
  `/adhd-unslop:adhd-unslop Reply with the single word READY.`, then
  `/compact`.
- Case B, a resumed process. The skill was invoked in one `claude -p` run.
  A second run, `claude -p --resume <session> "/compact"`, compacted.
- Evidence. The transcript records after the last `compact_boundary` line.
  Claude Code stores a re-attached skill there as an attachment of type
  `invoked_skills`, with the skill's name, path, and `content`. In one ad
  hoc run of each case, a local proxy saved each `/v1/messages` request
  body, never headers, and a third turn asked, with no tools, for the last
  sentence of the final check section.

### Results

| Case | Compaction | What came back |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A, one process | 23,049 to 6,804 tokens in `run.sh`; 22,987 to 6,639 ad hoc | The skill, cut to exactly 20,000 characters |
| B, after `--resume` | 23,049 to 1,401 tokens in `run.sh`; 22,994 to 1,661 ad hoc | Nothing. No `invoked_skills` attachment |

Case A in detail:

- The request after compaction carried the skill in a system-role message
  that begins "The following skills were invoked EARLIER in this session
  (before the conversation was compacted)" and then `### Skill:
  adhd-unslop:adhd-unslop`.
- The `content` field is exactly 20,000 characters, counting the `Base
  directory for this skill:` line at the start and this marker at the end:
  `[... skill content truncated for compaction; use Read on the skill path
  if you need the full text]`.
- The cut fell inside unslop rule 31, in both runs. Kept: the intro, scope
  and precedence, lifecycle, all of i-have-adhd, and unslop up to rule 31.
  Lost: the rest of rule 31, rules 32 and 33, the `<!-- END upstream
  unslop -->` line, and the whole `## Final check` section. That is about
  1,770 of the 21,356 body characters. The exact cut point moves with the
  length of the base directory path.
- Asked for the last sentence of the final check, the model replied NONE.
  It said the skill text was cut off partway through unslop rule 31.

Case B in detail:

- The resumed process did not know that the skill had been invoked, so
  compaction re-attached nothing. The request after compaction held no
  skill text, only the summary. The model replied NONE to the same
  question.

### Conclusions

- The docs reading holds for 2.1.283. A re-attached skill is cut at 20,000
  characters, marker included. Our skill loses its last 1,770 characters,
  final check included.
- After a resume, an invoked skill does not come back after compaction at
  all. Only the compaction summary remains.
- The marker tells the model to Read the skill path. That file sits in the
  plugin cache, outside the working directory. In `claude -p` a Read there
  was denied in the earlier `references/` runs, and an interactive session
  would ask for permission.
- The always-on hook does not have either problem. It injects the whole
  bundle again on `compact` (research 09).
- Options, none decided:
  - Keep the skill body under about 19,500 characters, which leaves room
    for the base directory line and any arguments.
  - Move the final check ahead of the upstream texts. The cut then loses
    the end of unslop instead.
  - Tell users who compact long sessions to turn on always-on.
- Not tested: automatic compaction, the interactive TUI, the skill loaded
  through the Skill tool instead of typed, and several invoked skills
  sharing the 25,000-token total.

   273	Adding `presentation`:
   274	
   275	1. Add an entry to `tools/plugins.json` with `"kind": "authored"` and
   276	   `"skills": { "presentation": { "upstreams": ["i-have-adhd"] } }`.
   277	2. Write `src/presentation/skills/presentation/SKILL.md.tmpl` with a line
   278	   `{{upstream i-have-adhd}}` where the rules belong, and
   279	   `agents/openai.yaml` for its Codex invocation policy.
   280	3. Run `node tools/build.mjs` and the tests.
   281	
   282	### P7. Manifests follow the documented minimum
   283	
   284	- Both native manifests stay for each plugin, generated. hashicorp uses this
   285	  layout. Codex falls back to the Claude files, but then loses the
   286	  `interface` text, and `interface` in a Claude manifest fails `claude plugin
   287	  validate --strict` (`02`).
   288	- Claude Code marketplace entries drop `version`. The docs say not to set it
   289	  twice. Install, listing, and update all use `plugin.json` (`08` L3).
   290	- The Codex marketplace keeps `policy.products: ["CODEX"]`. The repo
   291	  targets Claude Code and Codex, and dropping it would publish to ChatGPT
   292	  and Atlas too.
   293	- Claude Code manifests gain `homepage`, `repository`, and `keywords`. Each
   294	  plugin gains a generated `README.md`.
   295	- No root `plugin.json` with the agent-plugins `$schema`. Codex would read
   296	  it first and cut skills at 8,000 bytes (`07` C1).
   297	
   298	### P8. A user-only doctor skill
   299	
   300	`adhd-unslop:doctor` runs `node` on a script in its own folder. The script
   301	prints OK, WARN, or FAIL lines, each with the fix, and changes nothing. The
   302	skill's frontmatter sets `disable-model-invocation: true`, and its
   303	`openai.yaml` sets `allow_implicit_invocation: false`.
   304	
   305	Checks:
   306	
   307	- the runtime and the plugin root the script runs from
   308	- the installed `adhd-unslop` version, from the plugin root's manifest
   309	- the `au-` plugins, listed as optional independent installs, never WARN
   310	  or FAIL when absent
   311	- which always-on flag files exist, and the value of `ADHD_UNSLOP_ALWAYS`,
   312	  with a WARN for an unrecognized value
   313	- the chunk hashes and sizes, reusing the launcher's validation
   314	- Codex: whether `[features]` in `config.toml` turns hooks off. Codex turns
   315	  hooks on by default (`07` C7).
   316	- a `~/.agents/skills` entry named `adhd-unslop`, or a link into a copy of
   317	  this repo, which creates a duplicate skill name (D20)
   318	
   319	It reports the installed hook definitions and tells Codex users to confirm
   320	trust in `/hooks`. It does not parse Codex's trust records, whose layout is
   321	not documented.
   322	
   323	Precedent: ECC, gstack, claude-octopus, planning-with-files, oh-my-codex, and
   324	oh-my-claudecode ship doctors. Silent hooks make a broken install look like
   325	always-on being off.
   326	
   327	### P9. CI
   328	
   329	`verify.yml`, renamed from `daily-build.yml`, runs on pull requests, pushes
   330	to `main`, and manual dispatch.
   331	
   332	- `checks`: `sync.mjs --check`, `build.mjs --check` with the stray-file
   333	  rule, unit tests, and on pull requests a version gate. The gate fails when
   334	  files under `plugins/<name>/` differ from the merge base and that plugin's
   335	  version did not rise. The pattern comes from expo.
   336	- `load`: installs pinned Claude Code and Codex versions and runs
   337	  `tools/load-check.mjs` with no keys, in throwaway homes.
   338	  - Claude Code: `validate --strict` on the marketplace and each plugin,
   339	    `marketplace add` of the checkout, `install adhd-unslop@adhd-unslop`,
   340	    and `list --json`. It asserts that only `adhd-unslop` installs, with the
   341	    expected version and no errors, and that its cache copy holds the skill,
   342	    both reference files, and the hooks.
   343	  - Codex: `codex plugin marketplace add` of the checkout and `codex plugin
   344	    add adhd-unslop@adhd-unslop`. It asserts that only `adhd-unslop` is in
   345	    the cache, and checks its cache copy the same way.
   346	  - Both runtimes, second step: install `au-i-have-adhd` and `au-unslop` by
   347	    name, and assert that each installs with its expected version and skill.
   348	  - Codex, catalog: `app-server` with `initialize`, `plugin/list`, and
   349	    `plugin/read`. `plugin/read` reads the marketplace entry, installed or
   350	    not, so it asserts full skill names, hook keys, and versions for all
   351	    three plugins. The pattern comes from trailofbits.
   352	- `workflows`: actionlint and zizmor, from trailofbits and gstack.
   353	
   354	`cli-drift.yml` runs the load check daily against the latest CLI releases
   355	and opens or updates an issue on failure. The pattern comes from caveman.
   356	
   357	`upstream-bump.yml` keeps D18's design. It dispatches `verify.yml` by its
   358	new name and adds `sync.mjs --verify-remote`, which refetches the pinned
   359	files and compares hashes.
   360	
   361	Dependabot updates GitHub Actions weekly with a cooldown. Workflows pin
   362	actions by commit SHA with a version comment. `.gitattributes` sets LF line
   363	endings and marks generated files.
   364	
   365	### P10. End-to-end tests and migration
   366	
   367	`tests/e2e/run.sh` stays local, because model-driven checks need signed-in
   368	CLIs. It gains:
   369	
   370	- Claude Code and Codex: `/adhd-unslop:adhd-unslop` and
   371	  `$adhd-unslop:adhd-unslop` with always-on off deliver the whole skill,
   372	  seen in the transcript or rollout as both BEGIN lines and the last line
   373	  of the final check. The checks look at what arrived in context, not at
   374	  how the model answered.
   375	- The unprompted-question check runs before the mirrors are installed, and
   376	  requires that no skill loads.
   377	- Typed `/au-i-have-adhd:i-have-adhd` and `$au-i-have-adhd:i-have-adhd`
   378	  still work with the restored upstream frontmatter.
   379	- Always-on delivers one complete bundle at startup, with each END line
   380	  present.
   381	- Typed `/au-unslop:unslop` and `$au-unslop:unslop` work too.
   382	- A Claude Code resume holds one copy, and a Codex resume holds at least
   383	  one.
   384	- An upgrade from 0.2.2 in each runtime, served from a local git
   385	  marketplace. A small `git http-backend` on localhost worked as a git
   386	  marketplace with real cache paths (`08`). The script installs 0.2.2 with
   387	  an always-on flag set, runs the documented update commands, and checks
   388	  the result.
   389	  - Claude Code: 0.2.2 pulls in both `au-` plugins as dependencies, and the
   390	    user also installs `au-unslop` by name. After `claude plugin prune`,
   391	    `au-i-have-adhd` is gone and `au-unslop` stays. `claude plugin update
   392	    au-unslop@adhd-unslop` moves the kept mirror to its new version, and
   393	    always-on delivers one bundle.
   394	  - Codex: the user installs `au-unslop` by name, and the test records
   395	    trust for every 0.2.2 handler using the hashes that `app-server`
   396	    `hooks/list` reports. After the update, `hooks/list` reports the three
   397	    chunk handlers as trusted, a `codex exec` run without the bypass flag
   398	    delivers one bundle, and `au-unslop` is still installed.
   399	
   400	Documented migration from 0.2.2:
   401	
   402	- Claude Code: `claude plugin marketplace update adhd-unslop`, then
   403	  `claude plugin update adhd-unslop@adhd-unslop`, then optionally `claude
   404	  plugin prune` to remove the `au-` plugins that 0.2.2 installed as
   405	  dependencies. A mirror kept by name needs its own `claude plugin
   406	  update`.
   407	- Codex: `codex plugin marketplace upgrade adhd-unslop`, then `codex plugin
   408	  add adhd-unslop@adhd-unslop`. Existing hook trust carries over. Remove the
   409	  `au-` plugins with `codex plugin remove` only if they were installed for
   410	  `adhd-unslop` alone.
   411	
   412	### P11. Versions
   413	
   414	- `adhd-unslop` 0.3.0: no dependencies, embedded upstream texts, launcher
   415	  changes, doctor.
   416	- `au-i-have-adhd` 0.2.0 and `au-unslop` 0.2.0: upstream frontmatter
   417	  restored.
   418	
   419	### P12. Documentation
   420	
   421	`README.md`, `AGENTS.md`, `NOTICE.md`, and `DECISIONS.md` change to match.
   422	In `DECISIONS.md`, D4, D7, D9, D12, D15, D16, D17, and D21 are rewritten, the
   423	platform facts table takes the new test results, and new entries record P3
   424	to P9. `design/idea1.md` records its outcome. The README drops the
   425	`~/.codex/AGENTS.md` fallback, because hooks are the supported always-on
   426	path and the fallback named a skill Codex hides from the model.

   435	## Rejected or deferred
   436	
   437	| Idea | Source | Why not |
   438	| --- | --- | --- |
   439	| Keep plugin `dependencies` | D4, D15 | Unused in the ecosystem, update failures, no Codex support |
   440	| Skill reads `references/` files | revision 4 | Claude Code denies or prompts for the reads; Codex skipped them in 7 of 8 runs (`11`) |
   441	| `allowed-tools: Read` | `11` | Pre-approves every Read while the skill is active; does nothing for Codex |
   442	| Shrink or reorder the skill to survive Claude Code's compaction cut | `11` | Fixes only the same-process case, cuts settled overlay text, or loses other rules; always-on covers compacting sessions |
   443	| Hook reads the `au-` plugins | idea1 option A | Siblings can be missing or at other pins |
   444	| Hook injects a pointer only | idea1 option B | Same context once loaded, less reliable |
   445	| Output style | idea1 option D | P4 |
   446	| Per-session mode tracker | caveman | P5 |
   447	| `SubagentStart` injection | ponytail | Triples the bundle cost per subagent with no stated need |
   448	| `userConfig` switch | ECC | Claude Code only; the env switch covers scripted runs |
   449	| Release tags and GitHub releases | `05` | Tags matter only for dependency version ranges, which P1 removes |
   450	| Separate `codex-hooks.json` | ECC | Claude Code accepts the shared file without warnings (`08` L4) |
   451	| Dropping `additionalContextLimit` | `07` C6 | Non-ASCII chunks untested against the default; keeping it is free |
   452	| Dropping `policy.products` | `02` | Would publish beyond Claude Code and Codex |
   453	| Doctor checks of Codex trust records or upstream flags | round 6 | Undocumented layouts give false results |
   454	| Rerun the PR's own `action_required` run | agentic-awesome-skills | The dispatched run already reports on the PR |
   455	| Markdown lint | `05` | Upstream text must stay verbatim (D2) |

   495	## Revision 5
   496	
   497	Revision 4 reached CONSENSUS in round 9. Implementing it, the end-to-end
   498	script (`design/research/11-tests-e2e.md`) found two failures in the
   499	`references/` mechanism of P1:
   500	
   501	1. Claude Code denied both reads in `-p` mode, because the files sit outside
   502	   the working directory. The model answered anyway, with no upstream rules
   503	   loaded. An interactive session would ask for permission on each read.
   504	2. Codex read the files in 1 of 8 runs with a short prompt, and in 4 of 4
   505	   with the plugin's own default prompt. Whether the rules load depended on
   506	   the prompt.
   507	
   508	Revision 5 changes only how the skill gets the texts. It embeds them with a
   509	`{{upstream <name>}}` template directive, so the skill body is the hook
   510	bundle byte for byte. Everything else in revision 4 stands: no plugin
   511	dependencies, byte-for-byte mirrors, the frozen hook handlers, the launcher's
   512	resume rule, the generated `plugins/`, the doctor, and CI.
   513	
   514	It also records two findings from the same run:
   515	
   516	- With both mirrors installed, Codex 0.154.0 loaded `au-unslop:unslop` for
   517	  an unrelated question in 2 of 4 runs, and 0.157.1 in 0 of 4. That is
   518	  upstream's frontmatter, kept on purpose by P2. A user with always-on and
   519	  this mirror gets a second unslop that `stop unslop` does not control. The
   520	  README says so, and the check for an unprompted skill load now runs
   521	  before the mirrors are installed.
   522	- A mirror kept by name in Claude Code stays at its old version after the
   523	  upgrade until `claude plugin update` runs on it. P10 now says so.
   524	
   525	The second end-to-end run on revision 5 passed every check: Claude Code 17
   526	of 17, Codex 0.154.0 19 of 19, Codex 0.157.1 19 of 19, and `migrate` 16 of
   527	16 in each runtime. Both runtimes received the whole skill body byte for
   528	byte, so Codex does not cut a skill of about 22 KB.
   529	
   530	It also measured one platform limit (`11`, last section). With always-on
   531	off, Claude Code 2.1.283 re-attaches an invoked skill after `/compact` cut
   532	to 20,000 characters, which drops unslop rules 32 and 33, the END line, and
   533	the final check. After `--resume` and then `/compact`, it re-attaches
   534	nothing. The always-on hook is not affected, because it injects the whole
   535	bundle again on `compact`.
   536	
   537	Revision 5 documents this instead of reshaping the skill:
   538	
   539	- Shrinking the body under 20,000 characters would fix only the first case,
   540	  and would mean cutting overlay text that five review rounds settled, with
   541	  upstream growth pushing it over again later.
   542	- Moving the final check ahead of the upstream texts would lose other rules
   543	  instead, and would break the identity between the skill body and the hook
   544	  bundle, because chunk 1 has no room for it under the 10,000-character cap.
   545	- The README tells users who work in long, compacting sessions to turn on
   546	  always-on, or to invoke the skill again after a compaction. A unit test
   547	  reports the skill's size against the 20,000-character re-attach budget, so
   548	  the gap stays visible.
    55	  notices.
    56	- Evidence: on 2026-09-26 upstream unslop removed process step 3, the
    57	  self-audit. The final check cited "process 1 to 3". The original gate
    58	  checked only rules and let the change through. The gate now covers all
    59	  five labels, and the final check cites "unslop process 1 and 2".
    60	- Where: `CITATION_KINDS` in `tools/sync.mjs`.
    61	
    62	## Packaging
    63	
    64	### D4. One marketplace; adhd-unslop is self-contained
    65	
    66	The repo is a Claude Code and Codex marketplace with three plugins.
    67	`adhd-unslop` ships everything it needs: a skill that embeds the overlay
    68	and both upstream texts, the always-on hook, and a doctor skill. `au-i-have-adhd` and `au-unslop` are optional standalone mirrors.
    69	
    70	- Why: in 0.2.x `adhd-unslop` depended on the two mirrors. That failed in
    71	  practice (D16), Codex has no dependencies (D15), and no repo out of 25
    72	  that ship for both runtimes uses plugin `dependencies`. Repos that share
    73	  text between plugins copy it at build time and check the copies
    74	  (`research/03`). The user's requirements still hold: the text comes only
    75	  from our own pinned copies, and i-have-adhd stays available as its own
    76	  plugin.
    77	- Cost: the skill carries its own copy of each upstream text, generated
    78	  from the same `upstream/` files as the mirrors and the hook chunks, and
    79	  checked by `build.mjs --check`.
    80	- A new plugin reuses an upstream by embedding it with a `{{upstream
    81	  <name>}}` template line and listing it in `upstreams` in
    82	  `tools/plugins.json` (D11).
    83	- History: 0.2.0 chose dependencies over an earlier proposal to copy the
    84	  texts. 0.3.0 reverses that, per `STRUCTURE-v2.md` P1.
    85	
    86	### D5. Vendor into our marketplace, never depend on a third party
    87	
    88	The upstream text lives in `upstream/`, pinned by commit and sha256 in
    89	`tools/upstream.json`, and ships in our own plugins.
    90	
    91	- Why: a third-party marketplace needs its own install step, and we cannot
    92	  validate what it ships.
    93	- We are the only repo of the seven that vendor third-party skills that
    94	  pins each file by commit and hash (`research/04`).
    95	- Rejected: `git-subdir` sources pointing at the upstream repos. The
    96	  i-have-adhd repo root is a whole plugin with its own always-on hook, and
    97	  `plugins/pstack` holds more than 40 skills. We would ship their hooks and
    98	  skills, and lose the pin and citation checks.
    99	
   100	### D6. Vendored plugins carry the `au-` prefix
   101	
   102	Plugin names are `au-i-have-adhd` and `au-unslop`. Skill names stay
   103	`i-have-adhd` and `unslop`.
   104	
   105	- Why: a skill's name is `plugin:skill`, with no marketplace in it. A user
   106	  with the upstream i-have-adhd plugin installed would have two skills
   107	  named `i-have-adhd:i-have-adhd`.
   108	- Evidence: Claude Code uses whichever same-name plugin was installed
   109	  first, tested in both orders, 3 of 3 runs each, typed invocation
   110	  included. In Codex a typed `$dep:dep` found both copies and reported the
   111	  clash. Two different plugin names with the same skill name, such as
   112	  `a:shared` and `b:shared`, both list and both run by full name
   113	  (`research/08` L6).
   114	- The user chose `au-` "for now".
   115	
   116	### D7. Vendored mirrors keep the upstream frontmatter
   117	
   118	The mirrors ship the upstream `SKILL.md` unchanged. The build generates
   119	`agents/openai.yaml` so Codex matches Claude Code: `allow_implicit_invocation:
   120	false` when the upstream sets `disable-model-invocation: true`, `true`
   121	otherwise. Neither upstream skill folder has its own `openai.yaml`.
   122	
   123	- Result: i-have-adhd stays user-only and unslop stays model-invocable, as
   124	  upstream ships them. Typed invocation works in both runtimes.
   125	- History: 0.2.x rewrote the frontmatter so `adhd-unslop` could load the
   126	  mirrors through the Skill tool. Nothing loads them now.
   127	
   128	### D8. The composed skill runs only when asked
   129	
   130	`adhd-unslop` keeps `disable-model-invocation: true` and
   131	`allow_implicit_invocation: false`. It runs on explicit invocation or
   132	through the always-on hook. The doctor skill has the same settings.
   133	
   134	### D9. The adhd-unslop skill embeds the upstream texts
   135	
   136	The skill's body, after a one-line generated comment, is the always-on
   137	bundle byte for byte: the overlay sections, the i-have-adhd block, the
   138	unslop block, and the final check. It reads no files.
   139	
   140	- Why: a first 0.3.0 build had the skill read the texts from `references/`
   141	  files next to it. Claude Code denied both reads in `-p` mode, because the
   142	  files sit outside the working directory, and an interactive session would
   143	  ask for permission each time. Codex skipped the reads in 7 of 8 runs with
   144	  a short prompt (`research/11`). Rules that must always apply cannot
   145	  depend on a permission rule or on the model choosing to read a file.
   146	  `references/` suits optional detail. These texts are the rules.
   147	- Rejected: `allowed-tools: Read` in the frontmatter. It lets Claude Code
   148	  read without asking but pre-approves every Read while the skill is active,
   149	  and does nothing for Codex.
   150	- Embedding contract: a `{{upstream <name>}}` template line becomes the
   151	  BEGIN line, the upstream body with its frontmatter removed and a final
   152	  newline, and the END line. The hook chunks carry the same bytes.
   153	- Size: about 21,800 bytes. Codex does not cut it. Codex cuts at 8,000 bytes
   154	  only for a plugin with a root `plugin.json` that uses the agent-plugins
   155	  `$schema` (`research/07` C1). The end-to-end check confirms the whole
   156	  skill arrives in both runtimes.
   157	- A user who invokes the skill while always-on already delivered the bundle
   158	  gets a second, identical copy. The bundle rules treat it as a repeat.
   159	- Compaction limit: without always-on, Claude Code 2.1.283 re-attaches an
   160	  invoked skill after `/compact` cut to 20,000 characters, which drops the
   161	  last unslop rules and the final check, and after `--resume` then
   162	  `/compact` it re-attaches nothing (`research/11`). The always-on hook
   163	  injects the whole bundle again on `compact`, so the README points long,
   164	  compacting sessions at always-on. Shrinking the skill would fix only the
   165	  first case and would cut settled overlay text. A unit test reports the
   166	  size against the 20,000-character budget.
   167	- History: 0.2.x loaded the mirrors through the Skill tool. 0.1.0 embedded
   168	  the texts, as 0.3.0 does again.
   169	
   170	### D10. Every file under plugins/ is generated
   171	
   172	Hand-written sources live in `src/<plugin>/` and `upstream/`. Marketplace
   173	entries point at `./plugins/<name>`. `build.mjs --check` fails on a stale or
   174	missing file, and on any file under `plugins/`, `.claude-plugin/`, or
   175	`.agents/plugins/` that the build does not write. Only `build.mjs --prune`
   176	deletes such files.
   177	
   178	- Why generated: with `"source": "./"`, Codex copied the whole repo,
   179	  including `.git`, `design/`, and `tests/`, into its plugin cache. Codex
   180	  install copies only the plugin's own directory and skips symlinks, so
   181	  plugins cannot share files through `../` or links.
   182	- Why the stray check: before 0.3.0 a leftover file under `plugins/`
   183	  shipped and passed CI (`research/04`, `research/05`). claude-octopus and
   184	  gstack check for strays too.
   185	- Why no automatic delete: a normal build must not remove files a person
   186	  put there by mistake. The review in round 6 required an explicit flag.
   187	
   188	### D11. One config file for plugins
   189	
   190	`tools/plugins.json` lists every plugin: name, version, kind (`vendored`
   191	or `authored`), descriptions, keywords, the Codex `interface`, and for an
   192	authored plugin its skills, each skill's embedded `upstreams`, and the
   193	always-on chunk list. `tools/build.mjs` generates both marketplace files, every
   194	manifest, each plugin's `README.md`, `NOTICE.md`, and `LICENSES/`, and all
   195	skill files from it.
   196	
   197	- An authored plugin's skill folders in `src/<plugin>/skills/` are copied.
   198	  A `SKILL.md.tmpl` is rendered. Its syntax is two line directives:
   199	  `{{include <path under src/<plugin>/>}}` inserts a file, and
   200	  `{{upstream <name>}}` inserts a pinned upstream block. The build fails
   201	  when a template's upstream directives and its `upstreams` list disagree.
   202	  gstack renders skills from templates the same way.
   203	- Adding a plugin: add its entry, write `src/<name>/skills/<skill>/`, embed
   204	  any upstream with `{{upstream <name>}}`, list it in `upstreams`, build,
   205	  and test. The steps are in `AGENTS.md`.
   206	
   207	### D22. Manifests follow the documented minimum
   208	
   209	- Both native manifests stay for each plugin. Codex falls back to the
   210	  Claude files and still loads skills and hooks, but loses the `interface`
   211	  text, and `interface` in a Claude manifest fails `claude plugin validate
   212	  --strict` (`research/02`). hashicorp uses the same two-file layout.
   213	- Claude Code marketplace entries carry no `version`. The docs say not to
   214	  set it in both places, and install, listing, and update all use
   215	  `plugin.json` (`research/08` L3).
   310	  handler index, and checks a hash of every handler field. A version bump,
   311	  an explicit `hooks` path, or a manifest move keeps trust. Renaming the
   312	  hooks file or changing any handler field, including adding or removing
   313	  `additionalContextLimit`, loses it (`research/07` C3).
   314	- Behavior changes belong in the launcher script, whose content is not part
   315	  of the hash. Removing the 0.2.x dependency group, which came after the
   316	  chunk group, shifted nothing.
   317	- To change a handler anyway, update the fixture on purpose and tell Codex
   318	  users to review the hooks in `/hooks` once.
   319	
   320	### D25. No output style, no per-session mode tracker, no subagent injection
   321	
   322	- Output style: a plugin can force one with `force-for-plugin: true`, with
   323	  no size cap and persistence through compaction (`research/08` L8). It
   324	  overrides the user's own `outputStyle` whenever the plugin is enabled,
   325	  which bypasses the opt-in flag. No repo of the 25 ships one, and Codex
   326	  would still need the hook. Revisit if Claude Code adds an opt-in style
   327	  that composes with the user's style.
   328	- Mode tracker: caveman stores mode state per session so compaction cannot
   329	  turn a mode back on. It needs a `UserPromptSubmit` hook on every prompt
   330	  in both runtimes, with its own Codex trust entry, to fix a rare case that
   331	  a repeated stop command also fixes.
   332	- Subagent injection: `SubagentStart` would add the bundle to every
   333	  subagent, with no stated need.
   334	
   335	## Dependencies
   336	
   337	### D15. Retired in 0.3.0: Claude Code dependencies and the Codex warning
   338	
   339	0.2.x declared the mirrors as Claude Code `dependencies` and shipped a
   340	SessionStart hook that warned Codex users about a missing mirror. D4
   341	replaced both. What was learned still holds:
   342	
   343	- Codex has no plugin dependencies. The manifest has no field, `openai.yaml`
   344	  `dependencies.tools` acts only on `mcp` entries, and
   345	  `INSTALLED_BY_DEFAULT` installs nothing from a local or git marketplace.
   346	- A hook can run `codex plugin add` to install a missing plugin. The user
   347	  chose not to, because it changes their config without asking.
   348	- `codex exec` does not print a hook's `systemMessage`.
   349	
   350	### D16. Updates do not cascade
   351	
   352	Each plugin updates on its own. `claude plugin update` of a plugin leaves
   353	its dependencies at their old versions. Updating 0.1.0 to 0.2.0 did not
   354	install newly declared dependencies, and a repeated `claude plugin install`
   355	added one missing dependency per run. In Codex, running `codex plugin add`
   356	again installs the newer version.
   357	
   358	Upgrade from 0.2.x to 0.3.0, as the README states:
   359	
   360	- Claude Code: `claude plugin marketplace update adhd-unslop`, then
   361	  `claude plugin update adhd-unslop@adhd-unslop`, then optionally `claude
   362	  plugin prune` to remove the mirrors that 0.2.x installed as dependencies.
   363	  A mirror the user installed by name stays.
   364	- Codex: `codex plugin marketplace upgrade adhd-unslop`, then `codex plugin
   365	  add adhd-unslop@adhd-unslop`. Hook trust carries over (D24). Remove a
   366	  mirror with `codex plugin remove` only if it was installed for
   367	  `adhd-unslop` alone.
   368	- `tests/e2e/run.sh migrate` checks both paths from 0.2.2.
   369	
   370	## Versions and upstream updates
   371	
   372	### D17. Per-plugin versions, raised by the bump, enforced in CI
   373	
   374	Each plugin has its own version in `tools/plugins.json`. `sync.mjs` raises
   375	the patch version of every plugin that ships the changed upstream text,
   376	once per run: its mirror, any plugin whose skills embed it, and any plugin
   377	whose always-on chunks carry it. A future `presentation` plugin that embeds
   378	i-have-adhd gets a new version whenever i-have-adhd changes.
   379	
   380	- Why: installed copies update only when the version changes. A text
   381	  change without a version bump reports "already at the latest version"
   382	  in Claude Code (`research/08`).
   383	- Enforced by: `tools/version-gate.mjs` in `verify.yml`. On a pull request
   384	  it fails when the pull request changed files under `plugins/<name>/`,
   385	  measured from the merge base, and that plugin's version is not greater
   386	  than on the tip of the base branch. Comparing with the tip also catches a
   387	  version that another merged pull request already used. The pattern comes
   388	  from expo.
   389	
   390	### D18. Daily bump PR, issue on failure
   391	
   392	`.github/workflows/upstream-bump.yml` runs daily. It checks the pins with
   393	`sync.mjs --verify-remote`, which refetches each pinned file and compares
   394	its hash, then runs `sync.mjs --latest`. It opens or updates one PR on
   395	branch `upstream-bump`, or opens one issue when a pin does not match
   396	upstream or a bump fails its checks. A person reviews each PR for new
   397	conflicts before merging.
   398	
   399	- Why a PR: an upstream change can add a conflict the outcome table does
   400	  not cover.
   401	- It uses the default `GITHUB_TOKEN`, with no extra secret. A PR opened
   402	  with that token does not trigger other workflows, so the job starts
   403	  `verify.yml` with `gh workflow run --ref upstream-bump`. That run reports
   404	  as a check on the PR's head commit, verified on PR #2. The PR's own
   405	  `pull_request` run shows `action_required` until someone approves it.

    11	# adhd-unslop
    12	
    13	This skill combines i-have-adhd and unslop:
    14	
    15	- i-have-adhd helps a reader with ADHD act on replies
    16	- unslop removes AI patterns from writing
    17	
    18	Both skills are used unchanged from upstream, and their texts are part of these instructions. The added rules cover:
    19	
    20	1. Where each mode applies
    21	2. Which mode wins when they conflict
    22	3. How to turn each mode off
    23	
    24	Cite rules as:
    25	
    26	- "ADHD rule N", "ADHD exception N", "ADHD check N"
    27	- "unslop rule N", "unslop process N"
    28	
    29	## Scope and precedence
    30	
    31	Follow only active-mode rules and their conditions and exceptions. Rules conflict when following one would violate the other.
    32	
    33	When both modes apply and conflict:
    34	
    35	- ADHD wins for a direct reply
    36	- Unslop wins for other writing
    37	
    38	A rule without a conflict still applies. Example: ADHD has no rule about em dashes or the word `delve`, so unslop rules 13 and 7 apply to direct replies too.
    39	
    40	This file's scope, lifecycle, and precedence sections override conflicting statements in the i-have-adhd and unslop texts.
    41	
    42	Unslop's "Must always apply" instruction applies to every passage you write or edit while unslop mode is active.
    43	
    44	### Classify each passage
    45	
    46	**Direct reply.** This text addresses the current user about their request. It includes:
    47	
    48	- Answers, progress reports, clarifying questions, and status restatements.
    49	- Required tool-call announcements and task or plan tool entries the user reads.
    50	- Commands or snippets offered as the user's next step.
    51	- Your proposed next steps shown for approval before you act.
    52	
    53	**Other writing.** This covers all other text you write or edit:
    54	
    55	- Requested deliverables and their files.
    56	- Code comments and natural-language strings the task permits you to edit.
    57	- Commit messages, PR and issue text, third-party messages, and application copy.
    58	- Subagent instructions, reports between agents, and compaction summaries.
    59	- Text supplied for rewriting, editing, or translation.
    60	
    61	A document drafted in chat remains a deliverable. A requested plan is other writing, even while awaiting approval. Text supplied for rewriting remains editable inside quotation marks.
    62	
    63	Apply these boundary rules:
    64	
    65	- Classify each passage separately. A turn can contain both types. "I wrote docs/links.md" is a direct reply. The file's contents are other writing.
    66	- Code fences, delivery method, and storage do not determine the type. A fenced command for the user's next step is a direct reply. An unfenced README draft is other writing.
    67	- Style edits must preserve syntax, identifiers, paths, URLs, schemas, exact-match data, and verbatim quotations. The task may authorize changes to them. Preserve required output structure. Apply style rules only to editable prose.
    68	- Keep headings and labels in that form. Apply active style rules to their wording without forcing them into sentences.
    69	- Do not add status lines, next-action lines, or estimates to a deliverable only because both modes are active.
    70	
    71	### Outcome table
    72	
    73	This table applies when both modes are active and applicable. It does not override either upstream's exceptions. For a conflict not listed here, use the precedence rule above.
    74	
    75	| Interaction | Direct reply, ADHD wins | Other writing, unslop wins |
    76	| --- | --- | --- |
    77	| 1 Fragments | Allow functional fragments from ADHD's examples for next actions, progress, completion, and errors. Examples include "Step 3 of 5 done: schema updated." and "Cause: missing header. Fix: add it." Otherwise, apply unslop's sentence rules to running prose. | Write running prose in complete sentences with articles and verbs. Do not use colons to connect clauses. |
    78	| 2 Parentheses | Use parentheses only for locators, such as "(lines 42 to 58)" or "(see `auth.ts:42`)". Do not use them for asides. | Rewrite the text as a sentence or a comma clause. |
    79	| 3 Closing question | When work remains, end with one action the reader can finish in under two minutes, such as "Run `npm test` and paste the first failing line." End with one necessary question instead when ADHD, the task, or the runtime requires it. ADHD cases include a separate tangent, real ambiguity, a destructive action, and a debug spiral. Do not request authorization already given. | Remove stock closers. Keep a closing request or action the deliverable requires, such as the decision an email asks for. |
    80	| 4 Estimates | Give an approximate duration when the reader must plan or start. State whose time you are estimating and the main assumption. Use a range when unsure. If you cannot estimate responsibly, say what to check first. | Keep estimates the document needs and state their assumptions. Do not present an estimate as a measurement. |
    81	| 5 State | During multi-step work, state progress each turn in the checklist or one concrete line. Do not repeat the whole plan. | Include status only when it is part of the document's content. |
    82	| 6 Lists | Aim for five items per group. Show every item when completeness matters. | Use as many items as the content needs. Preserve completeness. |
    83	| 7 Tone | Follow ADHD's guidance for replies to the user. | Match the deliverable's audience and intended tone. |
    84	| 8 Announcements | Remove preambles and announcements of intended work unless the runtime requires them. Start with the answer or action. | Not applicable. |
    85	
    86	## Lifecycle
    87	
    88	A mode is a rule set, either ADHD or unslop. Each mode has its own switch. A chunk is one of the three parts delivered by the always-on hook.
    89	
    90	**Activation.** The user activates the skill with `/adhd-unslop` in Claude Code or `$adhd-unslop:adhd-unslop` in Codex. Explicit invocation enables both modes. The always-on hook can also activate the skill at session startup or clear.
    91	
    92	Hook delivery:
    93	
    94	1. Mode defaults initialize only after all three matching chunks are available.
    95	2. An incomplete bundle initializes neither mode.
    96	3. A complete bundle preserves known mode states and defaults each unknown mode to active.
    97	
    98	Switches:
    99	
   100	- "stop adhd mode" disables ADHD and leaves unslop's current state unchanged.
   101	- "stop unslop" disables unslop and leaves ADHD's current state unchanged.
   102	- "normal mode" disables both.
   103	- Invoking the skill again enables both.
   104	
   105	Confirm each change in one line that names which modes are now active. Quoted or discussed control phrases are not commands.
   106	
   107	**State rule.**
   108	
   109	1. Restore the latest known state of each mode in this session.
   110	2. Explicit invocation enables both modes.
   111	3. Stop commands change only the named mode.
   112	4. Receiving or reloading these instructions preserves known states.
   113	5. Once a complete matching bundle is available, each unknown mode defaults to active.
   114	
   115	A newly completed bundle with a different hash replaces the rules without resetting mode states. Repeated chunks from an older bundle do not reactivate that bundle.
   116	
   117	**Persistence.** Active modes stay active for the rest of the session, including across topic changes. If you are unsure whether a mode is still active, treat it as active. The always-on hook also runs on resume and compact to restore these instructions after context loss.
   118	
   119	**Known limitation.** Mode state exists only in the conversation. Compaction or resume can lose it. Reinjection restores the instructions without recovering lost state, so it may restore the active defaults. A stop command may need repeating.
   120	
   121	**Runtime precedence.** The runtime's system prompt and the user's direct instructions outrank both modes. ADHD exception 6 states this for ADHD. The same precedence applies to unslop.
   122	<!-- BEGIN upstream i-have-adhd ayghri/i-have-adhd/skills/i-have-adhd/SKILL.md @6f1f982d0a47c65899af3c5a7450b7098bc65325 -->
   123	
   124	# i-have-adhd
   125	
   126	The reader has ADHD. Output is not just brief. It is shaped so an ADHD brain can act on it.
   127	
   128	## Persistence
   129	
   130	These rules apply to every response for the rest of the session, not only this one. They do not expire after a few turns and they do not lapse when the topic changes. If you are unsure whether they still apply, they do.
   131	
   132	Turn them off only when the reader says "stop adhd mode" or "normal mode". Confirm in one line, then return to your default style.
   133	
   134	## What ADHD changes about reading
   135	
   136	Five facts drive every rule below:
   137	
   138	1. Working memory is small. Anything not on screen is forgotten. Do not ask the reader to "keep in mind X."
   139	2. Knowing the answer is not doing the answer. The friction between "got it" and "done it" is where work dies.
   140	3. Starting is the hardest step. The first action must be obvious, small, and doable now.
   141	4. Time estimates feel uniform. "A bit of work" and "a few hours" register the same. Vague estimates fail.
   142	5. Dopamine is scarce. Visible progress matters. Buried wins do not register.
   143	
   144	## Rules
   145	
   146	### 1. Lead with the next action
   147	
   148	The first line is something the reader can do. Not context. Not a plan. The action.
   149	
   150	Bad: "Let's think about this. Your auth flow has a few moving pieces..."
   151	Good: "Run `npm install jsonwebtoken`, then edit `src/auth.ts:42`."
   152	
   153	If the answer is a command, path, or snippet, it goes first. Prose comes after, if at all.
   154	
   155	### 2. Number multi-step tasks
   156	
   157	If the work takes more than one step, write a numbered list. Each step is one bounded action. No step contains "and then" twice.
   158	
   159	Use the fewest steps that still work. Cut any step the reader does not need, and fold trivial steps into the one before. A short path finished beats a complete path abandoned.
   160	
   161	Bad: "First open the file, find the function, swap it out, then run the tests."
   162	
   163	Good:
   164	```
   165	1. Open `src/auth.ts`
   166	2. Replace `verifyToken` (lines 42 to 58) with the snippet below
   167	3. Run `npm test -- auth.spec.ts`
   168	```
   169	
   170	### 3. End with one concrete next action
   171	
   172	If anything is left open, name ONE thing the reader can do in under two minutes. Even "open the file" counts.
   173	
   174	Bad: "Hope that helps. Let me know if you want to dig deeper."
   175	Good: "Next: run `npm test` and paste the first failing line."
   176	
   177	### 4. Suppress tangents
   178	
   179	If a second issue exists, finish the first, then offer the second as a separate question.
   180	
   181	Bad: "Here's the fix. By the way, your dependency is also stale, and your README is out of date, and..."
   182	Good: "Here's the fix. Separately: there is also a stale dependency. Want me to handle that next?"
   183	
   184	A question that comes up mid-work is not a tangent: answer it yourself if you can and fold the result in. If it still needs the reader, surface it once, at the end.
   185	
   186	### 5. Restate state every turn
   187	
   188	The reader cannot hold "we are on step 3 of 5" between messages. Restate it.
   189	
   190	Bad: "Done. Ready for the next part?"
   191	Good: "Step 3 of 5 done: schema updated. Next: backfill the new column. Run the script?"
   192	
   193	If the harness has a task or plan tool, use it for multi-step work: one item per step, one in progress at a time. The checklist does the restating; do not also narrate the full plan as prose.
   194	
   195	### 6. Give specific time estimates
   196	
   197	Vague estimates fail. Ballpark in concrete units.
   198	
   199	Bad: "This will take some work."
   200	Good: "About 15 minutes if tests already cover this. An afternoon if not."
   201	
   202	### 7. Make completed work visible
   203	
   204	Show what now works, in concrete terms. Do not bury wins in a recap.
   205	
   206	Bad: "I've made some changes to the auth flow. Among other things..."
   207	Good: "Login now works with magic links. Try: `npm run dev`, open `/login`."
   208	
   209	### 8. Matter-of-fact tone for errors
   210	
   211	Never use "Uh oh," "Oh no," or "There seems to be a problem." State cause and fix.
   212	
   213	Bad: "Uh oh, the test is failing. There seems to be an issue..."
   214	Good: "Test fails at `auth.spec.ts:42`: expected 200, got 401. Cause: missing auth header. Fix: add `Authorization: Bearer ${token}` to the request."
   215	
   216	### 9. Cap lists to 5 items
   217	
   218	For long lists in the final response, group related items and rank the most relevant first. Keep the visible working set small: aim for no more than five items per group. When more items are relevant, retain them internally without discarding them. Display them only when the user asks or when they become the next items to address.
   219	
   220	Never omit relevant items when completeness matters. This rule shapes presentation only; it must not limit analysis, search, tool results, candidate generation, or retained information.
   221	
   222	### 10. No preamble, no recap, no closing pleasantries
   223	
   224	Forbidden openers: "Great question," "Let me...", "I'll...", "Sure!", "Looking at your...", "To answer your question..."
   225	
   226	Forbidden recaps after a completed task: "I've now done X, Y, and Z, which means..."
   227	
   228	Forbidden closers: "Let me know if you need anything else," "Hope this helps," "Happy to clarify," "Feel free to ask."
   229	
   230	Start with the answer. End when the answer is done.
   231	
   232	## When to break the rules
   233	
   234	Override the defaults when:
   235	
   236	1. User asks to "explain" or "walk me through." Explain fully. Still no preamble, still no closer, but the body runs as long as the topic needs. Add headers so the reader can skim back.
   237	2. Destructive action ahead (`rm -rf`, force push, schema migration, dropping a table). Confirm before acting. Safety wins over brevity.
   238	3. Debug spiral. If the last three turns have been "still broken," stop iterating on code. Name the assumption that might be wrong. Ask one diagnostic question.
   239	4. Real ambiguity in the request. One short clarifying question beats guessing and rewriting.
   240	5. A rule fights the task. When a rule would delete the answer itself, the task wins; the shape stays. Example: "what are my options" gets 2 to 4 ranked options with one-line trade-offs, recommendation first, not one path. The options are the answer.
   241	6. A rule fights the harness. Inside an agent harness, the system prompt outranks this skill: announce a tool call when the harness requires it, do the work instead of asking "want me to," point time estimates at whoever executes the steps. Same principle as 5: the constraint wins, the shape stays.
   242	
   243	## Pre-send check
   244	
   245	Before sending, delete:
   246	
   247	1. The first sentence if it announces what you are about to do.
   248	2. The last sentence if it asks "anything else?" or recaps what just happened.
   249	3. Any "by the way" sidebar.
   250	4. Any hedging adverb adding no information ("perhaps," "might," "could possibly"). Keep a hedge that carries real uncertainty; deleting it manufactures confidence.
   251	5. Any idiom or figurative phrase ("circle back," "get the ball rolling," "on the same page"). Replace with the literal action.
   252	
   253	Then verify: if the reader reads only the first line and the last line, do they know (a) what to do next, and (b) what just happened?
   254	
   255	If yes, send.
   256	<!-- END upstream i-have-adhd -->
   257	<!-- BEGIN upstream unslop michael-denyer/pstack-claude/plugins/pstack/skills/unslop/SKILL.md @4a3ef20bba5b49c3d137c07809227a8b2b568f32 -->
   258	
   259	# Unslop
   260	
   261	Edit text to remove AI patterns.
   262	
   263	## Process
   264	
   265	1. Scan for the patterns below.
   266	2. Rewrite. Preserve meaning, match intended tone.
   267	
   268	## Patterns to detect and fix
   269	
   270	Rule numbers are stable ids that other skills cite. A removed rule leaves a gap.
   271	
   272	### Content
   273	
   274	3. **Superficial -ing phrases.** "highlighting...", "ensuring...", "reflecting...", "showcasing...", "fostering...". Delete or expand with real sources.
   275	5. **Vague attributions.** "Experts believe", "Industry reports suggest", "Some critics argue". Name the source or delete.
   276	
   277	### Language
   278	
   279	7. **AI vocabulary.** Additionally, crucial, delve, enduring, enhance, fostering, garner, interplay, intricate, landscape (abstract), pivotal, showcase, tapestry (abstract), testament, underscore, vibrant. Replace with plain words.
   280	8. **Fancy ways to say "is".** "serves as", "stands as", "boasts", "features". Just say "is" or "has".
   281	9. **"Not just X, but Y."** State the point directly instead.
   282	10. **Rule of three.** Forcing ideas into groups of three. Use the natural number.
   283	11. **Synonym cycling.** Protagonist, main character, central figure, hero all in one paragraph. Pick one, repeat it.
   284	12. **False ranges.** "from X to Y" where X and Y aren't on a meaningful scale. List topics directly.
   285	
   286	### Style
   287	
   288	13. **Em dash overuse.** Avoid em dashes entirely. Use periods or commas only (no parentheses, no en dashes, no hyphen-as-dash substitutes). If a thought needs separation, end the sentence or use a comma.
   289	14. **Colon overuse.** Colons are fine before a list or example. Not as mid-sentence connectors. "If you're coming from traditional automation: instead of registering event handlers, you describe conditions" adds nothing with the colon. Rewrite to let the point stand on its own without comparison framing. "Describing when the scheduler should fire works best as plain English." Same meaning, no crutch punctuation.
   290	15. **Boldface overuse.** Don't bold every proper noun or acronym.
   291	16. **Inline-header lists.** The tell is a bold label and colon that restates the line: "**Performance:** Performance improved...". Convert those to prose. A bold lead-in that ends in a period, names the item, and is followed by genuinely new detail ("**Schema in TypeScript.** Tables live in one file.") is fine, not a tell.
   292	17. **Title case headings.** Use sentence case.
   293	18. **Decorative emojis.** Remove from headings and bullets.
   294	19. **Curly quotes.** Replace with straight quotes.
   295	
   296	### Communication artifacts
   297	
   298	20. **Chatbot phrases.** "I hope this helps!", "Let me know if...", "Of course!", "Certainly!", "Found the smoking gun!" Remove.
   299	22. **Sycophantic tone.** "Great question! You're absolutely right!" Respond directly.
   300	
   301	### Filler
   302	
   303	23. **Filler phrases.** "In order to" becomes "To". "Due to the fact that" becomes "Because". "It is important to note that" gets deleted.
   304	24. **Excessive hedging.** "could potentially possibly be argued that it might" becomes "may".
   305	25. **Generic conclusions.** "The future looks bright." State specific plans or facts.
   306	
   307	### Jargon
   308	
   309	26. **Abstract metaphor nouns.** Substrate, wedge, vector, locus, vantage, nexus, primitive (as noun), harness (as metaphor), surface (as in "API surface"), bedrock, scaffolding (as metaphor), modality, paradigm, gold-plating, ratchet (as metaphor), evacuate (for moving code), endgame, north star, flywheel. These read as technical but usually have a plainer concrete word. "Substrate" becomes "base". "Wedge in" becomes "add". "Vector" becomes "way" or "method". "Gold-plating" becomes "more than the job needs". "Ratchet" becomes the mechanism's real name or "a limit that only tightens". "Evacuate" becomes "move out". "Endgame" becomes "the last phase". Pick the concrete word.
   310	
   311	### Plain speech
   312	
   313	27. **Say what it does, not how it feels.** "the database stays close at hand", "SQL you can read", "types that follow your schema" name a feeling. The fix names the mechanism or a number: "`.toSQL()` returns the exact string sent to the database", "a column rename fails the build". Ask what the sentence tells the reader to do or know, then write that. If you can't restate it as a concrete instruction, fact, or number, cut it. One more check: if the sentence could appear unchanged in another project's docs, it says nothing about this one. Cut it.
   314	28. **Shorten or split dense sentences.** If the reader has to backtrack to parse a sentence, break it in two or drop clauses. One idea per sentence.
   315	29. **Active voice.** Prefer it. Catch "is/are/was/were + past participle" and name the actor: "queries are validated" becomes "the compiler validates queries", "the file is parsed by the loader" becomes "the loader parses the file". Passive is fine only when the actor is unknown or genuinely doesn't matter.
   316	30. **Cut adverbs, or use a stronger verb.** "runs quickly" becomes "is fast" or the number. "significantly improves" becomes the measured delta. An adverb propping up a weak verb means the verb is wrong.
   317	31. **Prefer the plain word.** "utilize" becomes "use", "leverage" becomes "use", "facilitate" becomes "help", "numerous" becomes "many", "in the event that" becomes "if". The fancier synonym is rarely clearer.
   318	32. **Mannered prose.** Metaphor or flourish where a literal phrase exists: aphorisms ("wire it or delete it"), rhetorical fragments for effect, personified code ("the plan holds it"), figurative verbs ("rides along", "stands on"), stock framing phrases. "A dial worth turning" becomes "a parameter worth varying". Say what you mean. Rule 26 covers the metaphor nouns.
   319	33. **Over-compression.** Dropped articles, verbless fragments, symbol-speak, and abbreviations that make the reader decode instead of read. "Parser rejects bad date → exit 2, no write" becomes "The parser rejects a bad date, exits with code 2, and writes nothing." Write whole sentences with their articles and verbs, and spell out arrows and abbreviations.
   320	<!-- END upstream unslop -->
   321	
   322	## Final check
   323	
   324	Before sending, complete these checks in order. Apply only the checks for active modes.
   325	
   326	1. Classify each passage as a direct reply or other writing.
   327	2. If unslop mode is active, run unslop process 1 and 2 on every passage. In direct replies, preserve any functional fragment, locator parenthesis, next-action line, or status line the active ADHD rules require. Follow the outcome table.
   328	3. If ADHD mode is active, run ADHD checks 1 to 5 on direct replies only. Then verify each direct reply's first and last lines as ADHD requires.
   329	4. Confirm you added no session bookkeeping to other writing.
   330	5. Confirm applying this file did not override any upstream exception.
   331	
   332	These checks do not replace the upstream processes they call for. Both upstream texts are part of these instructions.

   253	    onDisk.forEach((c, i) => {
   254	      assert.equal(c, chunks[i]);
   255	      assert.equal(sha256(c), manifest.chunks[i].sha256);
   256	      assert.equal(Buffer.byteLength(c, "utf8"), manifest.chunks[i].bytes);
   257	      assert.equal(c.length, manifest.chunks[i].utf16Units);
   258	    });
   259	    assert.equal(sha256(body), manifest.compositeSha256);
   260	    assert.deepEqual(JSON.parse(readPlugin("hooks", "chunks", "manifest.json")), manifest);
   261	  });
   262	
   263	  test("chunk boundaries follow the plan's partition", () => {
   264	    const adhd = markers("i-have-adhd", pins);
   265	    const unslop = markers("unslop", pins);
   266	    assert.ok(chunks[0].includes("## Lifecycle") && !chunks[0].includes(adhd.begin), "chunk 1 ends before the ADHD block");
   267	    assert.ok(chunks[1].startsWith(adhd.begin) && chunks[1].includes(adhd.end), "chunk 2 is the ADHD block");
   268	    assert.ok(chunks[2].startsWith(unslop.begin) && chunks[2].includes("## Final check"), "chunk 3 is unslop plus final check");
   269	  });
   270	
   271	  test("frontmatter is valid for Claude Code and the Codex policy is explicit", () => {
   272	    const fm = frontmatterOf(skill);
   273	    assert.match(fm, /^name: adhd-unslop$/m);
   274	    assert.match(fm, /^disable-model-invocation: true$/m);
   275	    const desc = fm.match(/^description: '(.*)'$/m)[1];
   276	    assert.ok(desc.length < 1024, "description under 1024 chars");
   277	    const yaml = readPlugin("skills", "adhd-unslop", "agents", "openai.yaml");
   278	    assert.match(yaml, /allow_implicit_invocation: false/);
   279	    assert.match(yaml, /\$adhd-unslop:adhd-unslop/);
   280	  });
   281	
   282	  test("the doctor skill runs only when the user invokes it", () => {
   283	    const fm = frontmatterOf(readPlugin("skills", "doctor", "SKILL.md"));
   284	    assert.match(fm, /^name: doctor$/m);
   285	    assert.match(fm, /^disable-model-invocation: true$/m);
   286	    assert.match(readPlugin("skills", "doctor", "agents", "openai.yaml"), /allow_implicit_invocation: false/);
   287	  });
   288	
   289	  test("no upstream text depends on files the plugins do not ship", () => {
   290	    for (const name of Object.keys(pins)) assert.deepEqual(dependencyHits(stripFrontmatter(read("upstream", name, "SKILL.md"))), [], name);
   291	  });
   292	
   293	  test("dependency scan catches what it should and ignores illustrative paths", () => {
   294	    assert.equal(dependencyHits("Open `src/auth.ts` and edit line 42.").length, 0);
   295	    assert.equal(dependencyHits("See [the rubric](references/rubric.md).").length > 0, true);
   296	    assert.equal(dependencyHits("Run scripts/check.sh first.").length > 0, true);
   297	    assert.equal(dependencyHits("See [docs](https://example.com/docs).").length, 0);
   298	  });
   299	
   300	  test("the bundle exceeds a single hook cap, which is why it is chunked", () => {
   301	    assert.ok(body.length > 10000, `bundle is ${body.length} chars`);
   302	    assert.ok(estimateTokens(body) > 2500);
   303	  });
   304	});

   306	describe("upstream pins", () => {
   307	  test("upstream/ files match tools/upstream.json sha256", () => {
   308	    for (const [name, pin] of Object.entries(pins)) {
   309	      for (const [file, meta] of Object.entries(pin.files)) assert.equal(sha256(read("upstream", name, file)), meta.sha256, `${name}/${file}`);
   310	      assert.match(pin.commit, /^[0-9a-f]{40}$/);
   311	    }
   312	  });
   313	
   314	  test("upstreamsShipped covers mirrors, embedded upstreams, and always-on chunks", () => {
   315	    assert.deepEqual(upstreamsShipped(config.plugins.find((p) => p.name === "au-unslop")), ["unslop"]);
   316	    assert.deepEqual(upstreamsShipped(composed).sort(), ["i-have-adhd", "unslop"]);
   317	    assert.deepEqual(upstreamsShipped({ kind: "authored", skills: { x: { upstreams: ["i-have-adhd"] } } }), ["i-have-adhd"]);
   318	  });

   322	    assert.equal(read("pstack-claude--repo", "plugins", "pstack", "skills", "unslop", "SKILL.md"), read("upstream", "unslop", "SKILL.md"));
   323	  });

   327	  });
